### 'O pere e 'o musso

'O pere e 'o musso (Neapolitan: [o ˈpɛːr e o ˈmussə]; lit. 'the foot and the muzzle') is a typical Neapolitan dish.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Campania

Fun facts:

- (culture) The dish derives from popular tradition and a need to make use of less noble cuts of meat, and is usually sold as street food from carts, in the cities of Campania.
- (origin) 'O pere e 'o musso comes from Campania.

### Acqua pazza (food)

The term acqua pazza is used in Italian cuisine to refer to a recipe for poached white fish, or to simply refer to the lightly herbed broth used to poach it.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): fish, oil, tomato, garlic, parsley

Fun facts:

- (history) Acqua pazza became a very popular dish with tourists on the island of Capri in the 1960s, and as of the 1990s, it was the most popular way of preparing small whole fish in Campania.
- (about) In Campania in modern times, a version of the dish using fillets is sometimes made by first making a broth with fish head and trimmings.
- (inspiration) Many restaurants, such as the Acqua Pazza in San Marco, known for seafood and Neapolitan pizzas, and Acqua Pazza in Bologna, which specialises in seafoods and sauces, either share the name with or are named after this dish.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) An alternative explanation has "crazy" referencing the inclusion of chilis in the dish; Schwartz describes this as less likely, because he believes the dish likely predated the introduction of chilis and tomatoes to Italy in the early 16th century.
- (culture) There are many variations of this sauce, from light broths to thick tomato based sauces, which have been found on all types of seafood (not just the traditional white fish) and even chicken.
- (origin) Acqua pazza comes from Italy.

### Acquacotta

Acquacotta is an Italian broth-based bread soup that was originally a peasant food.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): bread

Fun facts:

- (origin) Its preparation and consumption dates back to ancient history, and it originated in the coastal area known as the Maremma, in southern Tuscany and northern Lazio.
- (about) Acquacotta was invented in part as a means to make hardened, stale bread edible.
- (origin) Acquacotta comes from Italy.

### Affogato

Affogato (), or more fully affogato al caffè, is an Italian dessert comprising a scoop of gelato, either fiordilatte (plain milk-flavored) or vanilla, topped with espresso.

- From: Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): espresso, gelato

Fun facts:

- (about) Another variation with a caramel espresso shot with vanilla ice cream is known as the Afragato.

### Agnolini

Agnolini is a type of egg-based stuffed pasta originating in the province of Mantua, Italy.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): stuffed pasta, egg pasta

Fun facts:

- (history) Agnolini's recipe was first published in L'arte di ben cucinare (1662) by Bartolomeo Stefani, a cook at the court of the Gonzaga family.
- (culture) Served with chicken broth, it is a traditional Mantuan dish on Christmas Eve, alongside other traditional Mantuan dishes such as the agnolini's soup, sorbir d'agnoli, with abundant addition of Parmesan cheese.
- (origin) Agnolini comes from Italy.

### Amaretti di Saronno

Amaretti di Saronno are a type of amaretto, a bitter-sweet flavored macaroon, traditional to the Italian city of Saronno.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Named after: Saronno
- Ingredients (Wikidata): Prunus dulcis

Fun facts:

- (inspiration) Amaretti di Saronno is named after Saronno.
- (culture) Amaretti di Saronno is one of many types of traditional amaretti, but the only one made with apricot kernels (the others are usually made with almonds).
- (origin) Amaretti di Saronno comes from Italy.

### Amatriciana

Amatriciana (matriciana in Romanesco dialect) is a sauce made with tomatoes, guanciale (cured pork cheek), pecorino romano or pecorino di Amatrice cheese, black pepper, dried chili pepper, extra virgin olive oil, dry white wine, and salt.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Named after: Amatrice
- Ingredients (Wikidata): white wine, table salt, tomato, oil, chili pepper, guanciale, pecorino, tomato sauce

Fun facts:

- (origin) Originating in the comune (municipality) of Amatrice (in the mountainous province of Rieti of the Lazio region), amatriciana is one of the best-known pasta sauces in present-day Roman and Italian cuisine.
- (inspiration) Amatriciana is named after Amatrice.
- (culture) The Italian government has named it a prodotto agroalimentare tradizionale (PAT) of Lazio, and amatriciana tradizionale is registered as a traditional speciality guaranteed (TSG) in the EU and the UK.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (origin) Amatriciana comes from Italy.

### Cacciatore

Cacciatore or cacciatora is an Italian dish prepared with meat, onions, herbs, usually tomatoes, often peppers, and sometimes wine.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (history) As of the 1990s in the southern Italian region of Campania, chicken cacciatore braised in white wine and tomato sauce was the most popular chicken dish.
- (about) Cacciatore is popularly made with braised chicken (pollo alla cacciatora) or rabbit (coniglio alla cacciatora), abbacchio (abbacchio alla cacciatora), an Italian preparation of lamb, capon (cappone alla cacciatora) or potatoes (patate alla cacciatora).
- (origin) Cacciatore comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) Various elements that distinguish the dishes have been put forward; the presence of a local wild variety of thyme called piperno, or the inclusion of rabbit intestines in the sauce, but by the 1990s, it was debated whether reproducing these elements was still observed.
- (about) A famous and very similar dish to rabbit cacciatore is eaten on the island of Ischia off the Gulf of Naples, named coniglio all'ischitana ('Ischian-style rabbit').

- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

### Cioccolato di Modica

### Crescentina modenese

Crescentine (sing.: crescentina), crescente (sing.: crescenta) or tigelle (sing. tigella) are thin, 10 centimetres (3.9 in) round breads from the Apennine Mountains in the Modena area of Emilia-Romagna, Italy.

Fun facts:

- (origin) Crescentina modenese comes from Italy.
- (culture) In the Apennines, crescente have long been eaten at home or enjoyed in traditional restaurants, but in the last decade some fast food and casual restaurants have added crescente to their menus.

### Crocetta di Caltanissetta

The crocetta di Caltanissetta is a traditional Italian sweet dessert that was produced in the comune (municipality) of Caltanissetta, Sicily, until the end of 1908, then forgotten about, and which has recently been rediscovered.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (culture) The crocetta di Caltanissetta and the spina sacra are made with a Marzipan dough that were traditionally prepared for the feast of the Most Holy Crucifix by the Sisters of the Benedictine Monastery.
- (origin) Crocetta di Caltanissetta comes from Italy.
- (culture) The rediscovery was possible after 20 years of research, that began with a person living in the neighbourhood who remembered how the traditional recipe was passed from mother to daughter over time.

### Fettuccine

Fettuccine is a type of pasta popular in Roman cuisine.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): flour

Fun facts:

- (history) A famous dish made with fettuccine is fettuccine Alfredo, which was created and named at a restaurant in Rome in the early 20th century as a tableside "performance".
- (culture) Fettuccine is descended from the extremely thin capelli d'angelo of the Renaissance, but is a flat, thick pasta traditionally made with egg and flour (usually one egg for every 100 grams or 3.5 ounces of flour).
- (about) Fettuccine is popular in the United States, where it is made with cream, although this is almost unknown in Italy.

### Frittole (meat dish)

Frittole di maiale (in Calabrian dialect frittuli, rarely in the singular frittula) is a traditional dish made of pork parts in the city of Reggio Calabria and the surrounding province.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (culture) Frittole is cooked slowly over embers of coal and stirred in the caddàra, the traditional tinned copper pot that is set up outside the butcher (traditionally on Saturdays) to serve the food freshly prepared so as to be consumed hot.
- (about) The typical popular saying "ru porcu not si jetta nenti" ('of the pig, nothing has to wasted') indicates that during all stages of the killing and slaughter, it takes food from all parts of the animal.
- (origin) Frittole comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (culture) The butchering of the pig, in Calabria, is a true collective ritual, of a liberating and at the same time propitiatory, during which the threat of natural forces is imprisoned in a symbolic ritual and culture.
- (culture) The frittole in Reggio Calabria, by tradition, are consumed during the Feast of Our Lady of Consolation, during which the aroma can be smelt around the old town, helping to create the colorful and distinctive festive atmosphere for the people.
- (culture) In Mammola, an ancient tradition survives of singing a serenade during the killing of the pig, sung and played with accordion and guitar to honor the family.

### Gianduiotto

Gianduiotto is a chocolate originating in the Piedmont region of Italy.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): Gianduja

Fun facts:

- (history) The official "birth" of gianduiotti was in 1852 in Turin, by Pierre Paul Caffarel and Michele Prochet, the first to grind hazelnuts into a paste before adding them to the cocoa and sugar mix.
- (inspiration) This blend itself is named after Gianduja, a mask in commedia dell'arte, a type of Italian theater, that represents the Piemonte.
- (origin) Gianduiotto comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) Mixing hazelnut into "standard" chocolates began at an industrial scale in response to Britain's blockade of Napoleonic France and its allies in the early 19th century, which greatly limited Italian access to South American cocoa.

### Neapolitan pizza

Neapolitan pizza is the version of the round pizza typically prepared in the Italian city of Naples and characterised by a soft, thin dough with high edges.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): mozzarella, tomato

Fun facts:

- (culture) Pizza napoletana is a traditional speciality guaranteed (TSG) product in the European Union and the United Kingdom, and the art of making it (arte del pizzaiolo napoletano) is included on UNESCO's list of intangible cultural heritage.
- (origin) Neapolitan pizza comes from Italy.
- (culture) The tomatoes are traditionally either San Marzano tomatoes or pomodorini del Piennolo del Vesuvio, which grow on the volcanic plains to the south of Mount Vesuvius, and the cheese is traditionally mozzarella di bufala campana or fior di latte di Agerola.

### Piadina romagnola

Piadina romagnola or simply piadina, traditionally piada, is a thin Italian flatbread, typically prepared in the Romagna historical region (which mostly consists of the Italian provinces of Forlì-Cesena, Ravenna, and Rimini, and the Republic of San Marino).

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Since: 1301-
- Ingredients (Wikidata): water, flour, olive oil

Fun facts:

- (history) Piadina romagnola dates from 1301-.
- (culture) The dough was traditionally cooked on a terracotta dish (locally called teggia or testo), although nowadays flat pans or electric griddles are commonly used.
- (about) Piadina has been added to the list of the prodotti agroalimentari tradizionali (PAT) of the Emilia-Romagna region, and to the protected geographical indication of the European Union in 2014.

- (origin) Pinzimonio comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) In areas of southern and central Italy (including Rome), it is known as cazzimperio.

### Pizza

Pizza is an Italian dish typically consisting of a flat base of leavened wheat-based dough topped with tomato, cheese, and other ingredients, baked at a high temperature, traditionally in a wood-fired oven.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): cheese, sugar, table salt, mozzarella, oil, dough, baker's yeast, bell pepper, tomato sauce

Fun facts:

- (about) The term pizza was first recorded in AD 997, in a Latin manuscript from the southern Italian town of Gaeta, in Lazio, on the border with Campania.
- (history) Raffaele Esposito is often credited with creating the modern version of pizza in Naples at the end of the 19th century.
- (culture) In 2017, the art of making Neapolitan pizza was included on UNESCO's list of intangible cultural heritage.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (culture) In 2009, Neapolitan pizza was registered with the European Union as a traditional speciality guaranteed (TSG) dish.
- (about) Pizza and its variants are among the most popular foods in the world.
- (origin) Pizza comes from Italy.

### Pizza quattro stagioni

Pizza quattro stagioni, also known as pizza 4 stagioni, pizza alle quattro stagioni, or la quattro stagioni, is a style of pizza in Italian cuisine that is prepared in four sections with diverse ingredients, with each section representing one season of the year.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): mozzarella, edible mushroom, tomato, globe artichoke, fiambre

Fun facts:

- (culture) Artichokes represent spring (a symbol of a new awakening), prosciutto represent summer, mushrooms represent autumn (a symbol of the autumn forest floor's abundance) and black olives represent winter.
- (about) Pizza quattro stagioni is a very popular pizza in Italy, and has been described as a "classic", "famous", and "renowned" Italian pizza.
- (origin) Pizza quattro stagioni comes from Italy.

### Sartù di riso

Sartù di riso is an Italian dish typical of the city of Naples, Campania.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): rice, egg as food, sausage, salami, liver as food, tomato sauce, smoked Provola, meatball, pea

Fun facts:

- (origin) Rice, a product imported by the Aragonese in the Kingdom of Naples, did not find success in south Italy—except in Sicily, where it was introduced by the Arabs.
- (about) Sartù di riso was probably born from the need to adapt this dish to the taste of the court, under the influence of the Austrian queen Maria Carolina of Austria.
- (history) Monsù, French court cooks, created this dish in the 18th century, enriching the rice with numerous ingredients and masking the flavor with tomato sauce.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) The very name of the dish may have come from the French surtout, the centerpiece that was used in the 20th century and that could also be used to bring sartù di riso to the table, cooked like a timballo.
- (culture) Sartù di riso is prepared with rice seasoned with ragù, peas, pancetta, mushrooms, fior di latte or provola, meatballs, sausages, boiled eggs and, traditionally, with chicken livers.
- (origin) Sartù di riso comes from Italy.

### Scaloppine

Scaloppine (plural and diminutive of scaloppa—a small escalope, i.e., a thinly sliced cut of meat) is a type of Italian dish that consists of thinly sliced meat, most often beef, veal, or chicken, that is dredged in wheat flour and sautéed in a reduction sauce, which varies by region.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (about) Popular sauces include tomato-wine reduction; scaloppine al limone or piccata, which denotes a caper-and-lemon sauce; scaloppine ai funghi, a mushroom-wine reduction; and pizzaiola, a pizza-style tomato sauce.
- (history) Veal scaloppine served with tomato sauce, prosciutto, and mozzarella has been popular in Italian-American cuisine since World War II, where a heavier version is made than in Campania.
- (origin) Scaloppine comes from Italy.

### Scialatielli

Scialatielli or scialatelli (Italian: [ʃalaˈt(j)ɛlli]), also known as sciliatielli or scivatieddi, is a short, thick pasta with a rectangular cross section and an irregular, slightly curved shape.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (history) The chef Enrico Cosentino devised the shape in the late 1960s in his native Amalfi while working in a local restaurant, and it gained recognition in 1978, when he won the Entremétier prize in an international culinary contest.
- (origin) Scialatielli is typical of modern Campanian cuisine, having originated on the Amalfi Coast as a chef's specialty, but it has also spread to nearby areas such as Catanzaro, Calabria, and Potenza, Basilicata.
- (about) Scialatelli is among the most recently invented pastas.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) By the 1990s, scialatelli was more often served with a seafood sauce.
- (origin) Scialatielli comes from Italy.

### Trofie

Trofie, less frequently troffie, strofie or stroffie, is a short, thin, twisted pasta from the Liguria region of Italy.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): durum wheat semolina

Fun facts:

- (origin) These thin, twisted shapes are believed to have originated in Sori, a town in the province of Genoa.
- (culture) Trofiette from Carloforte are also typical of the Sulcis archipelago in Sardinia, a region influenced by the Genoese culinary tradition known as cucina tabarchina (Tabarchino cuisine).
- (about) In 2019, Trofie di Sori received the De.Co. (Denominazione Comunale) designation, recognizing it as a product deeply tied to its local territory and community.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (culture) The pasta's distinct shape resembles wood shavings, traditionally referred to as risso da banché.
- (culture) This status aims to preserve not only the traditional production methods but also the historical and cultural significance of this distinctive pasta shape.
- (origin) Trofie comes from Italy.

### Tumact me tulez

- Ingredients (Wikidata): pasta

- (origin) Vitello tonnato comes from Italy.

### Zabaione

- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): sugar, egg as food

### Balsamic vinegar of Modena

Balsamic vinegar of Modena (BVM; Italian: aceto balsamico di Modena, ABM) is a protected geographic indication variety of balsamic vinegar.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (culture) Withdrawal and refilling, as used in making traditional balsamic vinegar, are not required; the ingredients, once mixed, must be kept in wood containers for a duration of at least 60 days.
- (about) The balsamic vinegar of Modena gained the PGI label on 3 July 2009.
- (origin) Balsamic vinegar of Modena comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (culture) The requirements for the much more expensive PDO Traditional Balsamic Vinegar are different and more restrictive; it must contain only grape must and be aged for at least 12 years.

### Barolo

- Origin (Wikidata): Piedmont
- Ingredients (Wikidata): Nebbiolo, Barolo riserva DOCG, Perno Barolo

### Brunello di Montalcino

Brunello di Montalcino is a red DOCG Italian wine produced in the vineyards surrounding the town of Montalcino, in the province of Siena, located about 80 km south of Florence, in the Tuscan wine region.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

Fun facts:

- (history) In 1980, Brunello di Montalcino was among the four wines awarded the first denominazione di origine controllata e garantita (DOCG) designation.
- (about) Brunello, a diminutive of bruno, is the name that was given locally to what was believed to be an individual grape variety grown in Montalcino.
- (origin) Brunello di Montalcino comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) In 1879 the province of Siena's Amphelographic Commission determined, after a few years of controlled experiments, that Sangiovese and Brunello were the same grape variety, and that the former should be its designated name.

### Cappuccino

Cappuccino is an espresso-based coffee drink traditionally prepared with steamed milk, including a layer of milk foam.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Named after: Order of Friars Minor Capuchin
- Ingredients (Wikidata): milk, espresso

Fun facts:

- (history) Cappuccino is sometimes said to have been served in the coffeehouses of Trieste and other Italian areas of the Austro-Hungarian Empire in the early 20th century, spreading throughout the Kingdom of Italy after World War I.
- (inspiration) Cappuccino is named after Order of Friars Minor Capuchin.
- (origin) Later, the Kapuziner was introduced in northern Italy during the period of Austrian domination, and Italians started to use it for the beverage as well as the friar dress.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) The Viennese bestowed the name Kapuziner, possibly in the 18th century, on an early version that included whipped cream and spices.
- (history) However, the existence in central Italy of a coffee drink mixed with milk named cappuccino is already documented in the 19th century.
- (origin) Cappuccino comes from Italy.

### Carmignano DOCG

Carmignano is an Italian wine region located in the Tuscany region and centered on the city of Carmignano, near Prato and about 10 miles (16 kilometers) northwest of Florence.

- From: Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): Cabernet Sauvignon, Sangiovese, Barco Reale di Carmignano DOC, Vin Santo di Carmignano DOC

Fun facts:

- (history) In 1975, the region was awarded Denominazione di origine controllata (DOC) status and subsequently promoted to Denominazione di Origine Controllata e Garantita (DOCG) status in 1990 (retroactive to the 1988 vintage).
- (culture) In the 1800s, the producers of the Carmignano region developed a tradition of blending Sangiovese with Cabernet Sauvignon, long before the practice became popularized by the "Super Tuscan" of the late 20th century.
- (history) Noted for the quality of its wines since the Middle Ages, Carmignano was identified by Cosimo III de' Medici, Grand Duke of Tuscany as one of the superior wine producing areas of Tuscany and granted special legal protections in 1716.

### Chianti

Chianti is an Italian red wine produced in the Chianti region of central Tuscany, principally from the Sangiovese grape.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Named after: Chianti
- Ingredients (Wikidata): Sangiovese, Chianti Rufina DOCG, Chianti Colli Aretini DOCG, Chianti Colli Fiorentini DOCG, Chianti Colli Senesi DOCG, Chianti Colline Pisane DOCG, Chianti Montalbano DOCG, Chianti Montespertoli DOCG, Vin Santo del Chianti DOC, Chianti Riserva DOCG

Fun facts:

- (history) In the late 19th century, Baron Bettino Ricasoli (later Prime Minister of the Kingdom of Italy) helped establish Sangiovese as the blend's dominant grape variety, creating the blueprint for today's Chianti wines.
- (origin) Other variants, with the exception of Rufina north-east of Florence and Montalbano south of Pistoia, originate in the named provinces: Siena for the Colli Senesi, Florence for the Colli Fiorentini, Arezzo for the Colli Aretini and Pisa for the Colline Pisane.
- (inspiration) Chianti is named after Chianti.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) The first definition of a wine area called Chianti was made in 1716.
- (history) Most of the villages that in 1932 were added to the newly defined Chianti Classico region added in Chianti to their names, for example Greve in Chianti, which amended its name in 1972.
- (history) In 1932 the Chianti area was completely redrawn and divided into seven sub-areas: Classico, Colli Aretini, Colli Fiorentini, Colline Pisane, Colli Senesi, Montalbano and Rùfina.
- (origin) Chianti comes from Italy.

### Chili oil

Chili oil or chile oil is a condiment made from vegetable oil that has been infused with chili peppers.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): People's Republic of China
- Ingredients (Wikidata): cooking oil

Fun facts:

- (about) Chili oil is particularly popular in Chinese cuisine, especially western Chinese cuisines, such as Sichuan cuisine, Hunan cuisine, Guizhou cuisine, and Shaanxi cuisine, where it is used as an ingredient in cooked dishes, as well as a condiment.
- (origin) Chili oil comes from People's Republic of China.
- (about) Recipes targeted to Western cooks also suggest other popular oils, such as rapeseed, grapeseed or peanut, and any dried or fresh chili peppers.

### Chili pepper

Chili peppers, also spelled chile or chilli, are varieties of berry-fruit plants from the genus Capsicum, which are members of the nightshade family Solanaceae, cultivated for their pungency (spicy heat).

- From: Italy

Fun facts:

- (origin) Chili peppers originated in Central or South America and were first cultivated in Mexico.
- (culture) European explorers brought chili peppers back to the Old World in the late 15th century as part of the Columbian exchange, which led to the cultivation of multiple varieties across the world for food and traditional medicine.

### Grappa

- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

- (origin) The original date grappa was created cannot be determined, but was likely first made pre-1300s as distillation arrived in Italy during the Middle Ages.

- (culture) In Uruguay, a local version called grappamiel has also been created, which sees honey added to the traditional grappa.

### Lardo

Fun facts:

- (about) The most famous lardo is from the Colonnata, Tuscany, where lardo has been made since Roman times.
- (culture) Colonnata is a frazione (hamlet) of the city of Carrara, which is famous for its marble; traditionally lardo is cured for months in basins made of Carrara marble, which is quarried in Colonnata.
- (about) Lardo di Colonnata is included in the Ark of Taste catalog of heritage foods and has been a protected geographical indication (PGI) since 2004.

### Limoncello

Limoncello is a traditional lemon liqueur produced mainly in Southern Italy, especially around the Gulf of Naples, the Gulf of Salerno, as well as in Sicily and in Gargano.

- Origin (Wikidata): Italy
- Ingredients (Wikidata): ethanol, drinking water, sugar, lemon zest

- (culture) Limoncello is the second-most popular liqueur in Italy and is traditionally served chilled as an after-dinner digestif.
- (about) Limoncello is also a popular homemade liqueur, with various recipes available online and in print.

### Parrina

- From: Italy

Fun facts:

- (history) The DOC was awarded in 1971, and extends to 110.33 hectares (272.6 acres) with about 20 wine producers, the largest of which, by far, is Antica Fattoria La Parrina.

- (history) The DOC originally allowed only the use of Italian varieties such as Trebbiano, Sangiovese and Ansonica, but an amendment in 1986 recognised emerging trends in allowing the use of Chardonnay, Cabernet Sauvignon and Merlot.

### Prosecco

Prosecco is an Italian DOC or DOCG white wine produced in a large area spanning nine provinces in the Veneto and Friuli-Venezia Giulia regions, and named after the village of Prosecco, in the province of Trieste, Italy.

- From: Italy
- Origin (Wikidata): Italy

- (culture) In 2019, Le Colline del Prosecco di Conegliano e Valdobbiadene (Conegliano and Valdobbiadene Prosecco Hills) became a UNESCO World Heritage Site, in large part due to the region's role in the production of Prosecco.
- (about) Prosecco is made from the Prosecco grape (renamed "Glera" in Italy in 2009), but denomination rules allow up to 15% of the wine to be other permitted varieties.
- (origin) Prosecco comes from Italy.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) Since 2020, the DOC rules allow a rosé variety of Prosecco designated spumante rosé, which must contain Glera blended with 10–15% Pinot nero.

### Zucchini

Zucchini ( zoo-KEE-nee; pl. zucchini or zucchinis; in Australia and North America), courgette ( koor-ZHET; in France and Britain), or Cucurbita pepo var. cylindrica is a summer squash, a vining herbaceous plant whose fruit are harvested when their immature seeds and epicarp (rind) are still soft and edible.

- From: Italy

Fun facts:

- (history) Zucchini descend from squashes first domesticated in Mexico and Central America over 7,000 years ago, but the zucchini itself was bred in Milan in the late 19th century.
